========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… the main thing is I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s getting in the way of work, mostly. I work at a bench with jewelry and small pieces, so normally I have to be really locked in, you know—like following a work order, checking a saw mark, answering a customer email, making sure I don’t miss some tiny detail.

But lately I’ll start on one thing, then I’m suddenly looking at an email, then I notice a mark on a piece, then some noise outside catches me, and then I look back and I’ve completely lost where I was in the work order. It’s like my attention just keeps jumping around before I can finish anything. That’s what made me think I should come in, because it’s not just annoying—it’s actually messing with my work.

It’s like my attention just keeps jumping around before I can finish anything. Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m normally active at work because it’s hands-on, but this was different. I couldn’t just sit at the bench and stay with one piece. And this wasn’t just during normal hours. I’d still be doing it after closing, like I knew I should lock up, but I’d keep finding one more thing to move, one more idea to jot down, one more message to send. It felt like I had this motor in me and sitting still at the bench felt almost impossible.

That’s what made me think I should come in, because it’s not just annoying—it’s actually messing with my work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with keeping your mind on one thing?\n\nPatient: It really kicked in right after that jewelry-show award I got. Like, before that I’d have scattered days here and there, sure, but this was different — it felt like my brain got switched on too high and I couldn’t bring it back down. That stretch went on for about two weeks, maybe right around there. Two weeks of feeling wired and kind of out of control, and that’s when the trouble keeping my mind on one thing became really noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more active or restless than usual since your attention problems started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m normally active at work because it’s hands-on, but this was different. I couldn’t just sit at the bench and stay with one piece. I’d sit down to work on a setting or check a detail, and within minutes I’d be up again or grabbing my sketchbook because I suddenly had an idea for a new watch case. Then before that was even halfway sketched, I’d be emailing suppliers about materials, then I’d look over and decide the display trays looked wrong and start rearranging them.\n\nAnd this wasn’t just during normal hours. I’d still be doing it after closing, like I knew I should lock up, but I’d keep finding one more thing to move, one more idea to jot down, one more message to send. It felt like I had this motor in me and sitting still at the bench felt almost impossible.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been making impulsive decisions since your attention problems started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it has. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just something I noticed at work, but it’s started spilling over into everything.

The biggest thing was I missed a major custom delivery — like, a really important one, not just a small repair pickup or something I could smooth over easily. I had been moving between tasks and notes and little details, and somehow the whole thing got away from me. That’s not normal for me. I’m usually pretty careful with deadlines because people trust me with expensive and personal pieces, you know? And then my shop records got so messy — invoices, job notes, order details, all of it — that I basically had to stop taking new work for now because I couldn’t trust that I’d keep it straight.

With family, yeah, they’ve noticed. They’ve been checking on me more than usual, not in a casual “how are you” way, more like they’re worried. I think they could tell I’d slipped pretty far from my normal routines. I wasn’t keeping things organized the way I usually do, and I think that scared them a bit. So yes, it’s caused problems — maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.

Yeah, most of my life I haven’t been like this. I mean, I’ve always been busy and I can get absorbed in my work, but this specific wired, out-of-control feeling wasn’t just my usual personality.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… the main thing is I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s getting in the way of work, mostly. I work at a bench with jewelry and small pieces, so normally I have to be really locked in, you know—like following a work order, checking a saw mark, answering a customer email, making sure I don’t miss some tiny detail.\n\nBut lately I’ll start on one thing, then I’m suddenly looking at an email, then I notice a mark on a piece, then some noise outside catches me, and then I look back and I’ve completely lost where I was in the work order. It’s like my attention just keeps jumping around before I can finish anything. That’s what made me think I should come in, because it’s not just annoying—it’s actually messing with my work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with keeping your mind on one thing?\n\nPatient: It really kicked in right after that jewelry-show award I got. Like, before that I’d have scattered days here and there, sure, but this was different — it felt like my brain got switched on too high and I couldn’t bring it back down. That stretch went on for about two weeks, maybe right around there. Two weeks of feeling wired and kind of out of control, and that’s when the trouble keeping my mind on one thing became really noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more active or restless than usual since your attention problems started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m normally active at work because it’s hands-on, but this was different. I couldn’t just sit at the bench and stay with one piece. I’d sit down to work on a setting or check a detail, and within minutes I’d be up again or grabbing my sketchbook because I suddenly had an idea for a new watch case. Then before that was even halfway sketched, I’d be emailing suppliers about materials, then I’d look over and decide the display trays looked wrong and start rearranging them.\n\nAnd this wasn’t just during normal hours. I’d still be doing it after closing, like I knew I should lock up, but I’d keep finding one more thing to move, one more idea to jot down, one more message to send. It felt like I had this motor in me and sitting still at the bench felt almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you been making impulsive decisions since your attention problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. Like, if you mean am I doing huge reckless things without thinking, I don’t think so… at least nothing that jumps out to me like, “Oh yeah, that was obviously impulsive.”\n\nI have been kind of jumping from thing to thing, and sometimes I’ll decide suddenly that something needs to be done right now, but whether that’s impulsive or just me being scattered and over-focused on whatever catches my attention, I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way. My main problem has felt more like I can’t keep my attention steady once I start something.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness been causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just something I noticed at work, but it’s started spilling over into everything.\n\nThe biggest thing was I missed a major custom delivery — like, a really important one, not just a small repair pickup or something I could smooth over easily. I had been moving between tasks and notes and little details, and somehow the whole thing got away from me. That’s not normal for me. I’m usually pretty careful with deadlines because people trust me with expensive and personal pieces, you know? And then my shop records got so messy — invoices, job notes, order details, all of it — that I basically had to stop taking new work for now because I couldn’t trust that I’d keep it straight.\n\nWith family, yeah, they’ve noticed. They’ve been checking on me more than usual, not in a casual “how are you” way, more like they’re worried. I think they could tell I’d slipped pretty far from my normal routines. I wasn’t keeping things organized the way I usually do, and I think that scared them a bit. So yes, it’s caused problems — maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where you didn't have these attention and restlessness problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, most of my life I haven’t been like this. I mean, I’ve always been busy and I can get absorbed in my work, but this specific wired, out-of-control feeling wasn’t just my usual personality.\n\nIt really started right after that jewelry-show award. I remember because it felt like everything suddenly sped up after that — like my head and my body both shifted into this different gear. From there it went on for about two weeks, give or take. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not more than six months. That’s actually part of why it feels so strange to me — this wasn’t some long-running pattern I can trace back forever.

The wired, out-of-control stretch really started right after the jewelry-show award, and it went on for about two weeks. During that time it felt very intense, like I couldn’t get myself settled or keep my attention where it needed to be, but it wasn’t something that had been happening for months and months before that.

I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way. My main problem has felt more like I can’t keep my attention steady once I start something.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness been causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just something I noticed at work, but it’s started spilling over into everything.\n\nThe biggest thing was I missed a major custom delivery — like, a really important one, not just a small repair pickup or something I could smooth over easily. I had been moving between tasks and notes and little details, and somehow the whole thing got away from me. That’s not normal for me. I’m usually pretty careful with deadlines because people trust me with expensive and personal pieces, you know? They’ve been checking on me more than usual, not in a casual “how are you” way, more like they’re worried. I think they could tell I’d slipped pretty far from my normal routines. I wasn’t keeping things organized the way I usually do, and I think that scared them a bit. So yes, it’s caused problems — maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where you didn't have these attention and restlessness problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, most of my life I haven’t been like this. During that stretch was when the attention problems and the restlessness felt really hard to control. Yeah… since that main two-week stretch ended, I’d say the worst of it isn’t like it was, but I still notice this thing with my attention, especially at work.

Like, I’ll be at the bench trying to finish a piece, and I’ll start checking a customer email, then suddenly I’m looking at a saw mark I left, then some noise outside catches me, and then I come back and realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the work I do needs focus. You can’t really be half in, half out when you’re dealing with small details and expensive materials. Yeah, pretty much nearly every day. It’s not always the exact same level every single day, but most days I notice it, especially when I’m at the bench trying to work.

That’s really what brought me in — my mind just won’t stay on one thing the way it should. I’ll be working on a piece, then I jump to a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny detail I feel like I need to fix, then a noise outside pulls my attention, and by the time I look back down, I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. Then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, what step was I on, what was I supposed to do next, did I already check this part?

So yes, I’d say it’s happening nearly every day, because my work makes it really obvious. If I had a different job maybe I could ignore it more, but with jewelry work, every little lapse stands out.

Like, before that I’d have scattered days here and there, sure, but this was different — it felt like my brain got switched on too high and I couldn’t bring it back down. That stretch went on for about two weeks, maybe right around there. Two weeks of feeling wired and kind of out of control, and that’s when the trouble keeping my mind on one thing became really noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more active or restless than usual since your attention problems started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m normally active at work because it’s hands-on, but this was different. I couldn’t just sit at the bench and stay with one piece. I’d sit down to work on a setting or check a detail, and within minutes I’d be up again or grabbing my sketchbook because I suddenly had an idea for a new watch case. Then before that was even halfway sketched, I’d be emailing suppliers about materials, then I’d look over and decide the display trays looked wrong and start rearranging them.\n\nAnd this wasn’t just during normal hours. I’d still be doing it after closing, like I knew I should lock up, but I’d keep finding one more thing to move, one more idea to jot down, one more message to send. It felt like I had this motor in me and sitting still at the bench felt almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you been making impulsive decisions since your attention problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. Like, if you mean am I doing huge reckless things without thinking, I don’t think so… at least nothing that jumps out to me like, “Oh yeah, that was obviously impulsive.”\n\nI have been kind of jumping from thing to thing, and sometimes I’ll decide suddenly that something needs to be done right now, but whether that’s impulsive or just me being scattered and over-focused on whatever catches my attention, I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way. My main problem has felt more like I can’t keep my attention steady once I start something.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness been causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just something I noticed at work, but it’s started spilling over into everything.\n\nThe biggest thing was I missed a major custom delivery — like, a really important one, not just a small repair pickup or something I could smooth over easily. I had been moving between tasks and notes and little details, and somehow the whole thing got away from me. That’s not normal for me. I’m usually pretty careful with deadlines because people trust me with expensive and personal pieces, you know? And then my shop records got so messy — invoices, job notes, order details, all of it — that I basically had to stop taking new work for now because I couldn’t trust that I’d keep it straight.\n\nWith family, yeah, they’ve noticed. They’ve been checking on me more than usual, not in a casual “how are you” way, more like they’re worried. I think they could tell I’d slipped pretty far from my normal routines. I wasn’t keeping things organized the way I usually do, and I think that scared them a bit. So yes, it’s caused problems — maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where you didn't have these attention and restlessness problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, most of my life I haven’t been like this. I mean, I’ve always been busy and I can get absorbed in my work, but this specific wired, out-of-control feeling wasn’t just my usual personality.\n\nIt really started right after that jewelry-show award. I remember because it felt like everything suddenly sped up after that — like my head and my body both shifted into this different gear. From there it went on for about two weeks, give or take. During that stretch was when the attention problems and the restlessness felt really hard to control. Before that, I could sit and work normally, keep things in order, and not feel like I was being pulled in ten directions at once.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness lasted for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. That’s actually part of why it feels so strange to me — this wasn’t some long-running pattern I can trace back forever.\n\nThe wired, out-of-control stretch really started right after the jewelry-show award, and it went on for about two weeks. During that time it felt very intense, like I couldn’t get myself settled or keep my attention where it needed to be, but it wasn’t something that had been happening for months and months before that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely hasn’t been six months. I know I keep coming back to the same point, but that’s honestly how I’ve been marking it in my head — it started right after the jewelry-show award. That was the clear turning point for me.\n\nFrom there, that wired, out-of-control stretch went on for about two weeks. It felt intense while it was happening, like it took over my normal way of functioning, but it wasn’t something that had been there for half a year or anything close to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms of inattention or restlessness since the initial two-week period ended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since that main two-week stretch ended, I’d say the worst of it isn’t like it was, but I still notice this thing with my attention, especially at work.\n\nWhat really bothers me — and honestly what made me feel like I needed to come in — is that my mind won’t stay on one thing the way it used to. Like, I’ll be at the bench trying to finish a piece, and I’ll start checking a customer email, then suddenly I’m looking at a saw mark I left, then some noise outside catches me, and then I come back and realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the work I do needs focus. You can’t really be half in, half out when you’re dealing with small details and expensive materials. So, yes, I have still had some of that inattention or restlessness, just not as intense as that first stretch.\n\nDoctor: Are you still experiencing difficulties with attention and restlessness now?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Not like the worst part before, but it’s still there enough that it’s messing with me.\n\nThe main thing is my attention. What really brought me in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. At the bench, I’ll be trying to work through an order, and then I’m checking a customer email, then I notice some little saw mark or detail on a piece, then a noise outside pulls me away, and when I come back I realize I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing. Like I have to rebuild the whole work order in my head again.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because that’s not how I usually work. My job kind of depends on being precise and steady, so even if I’m physically sitting there, my mind keeps jumping around. That’s the part that still feels very much present right now.\n\nDoctor: Are your current symptoms of inattention something that you experience nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much nearly every day. It’s not always the exact same level every single day, but most days I notice it, especially when I’m at the bench trying to work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in — my mind just won’t stay on one thing the way it should. I’ll be working on a piece, then I jump to a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny detail I feel like I need to fix, then a noise outside pulls my attention, and by the time I look back down, I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. Then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, what step was I on, what was I supposed to do next, did I already check this part?\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s happening nearly every day, because my work makes it really obvious. If I had a different job maybe I could ignore it more, but with jewelry work, every little lapse stands out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

That’s the part that still feels very much present right now.\n\nDoctor: Are your current symptoms of inattention something that you experience nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much nearly every day. It’s not always the exact same level every single day, but most days I notice it, especially when I’m at the bench trying to work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in — my mind just won’t stay on one thing the way it should. I’ll be working on a piece, then I jump to a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny detail I feel like I need to fix, then a noise outside pulls my attention, and by the time I look back down, I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. Then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, what step was I on, what was I supposed to do next, did I already check this part?\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s happening nearly every day, because my work makes it really obvious. If I had a different job maybe I could ignore it more, but with jewelry work, every little lapse stands out.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms of inattention been present for at least one month?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Manic_Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"What really brought me in is that my mind won’t stay on one thing anymore—at the bench I jump from a customer email to a saw mark to a noise outside and then I realize I’ve lost the whole work order.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has. I mean, it’s been long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad couple of days or me being distracted because of one stressful thing.

For at least a month now, my attention has just felt off. What really made me come in is that my mind won’t stay on one thing anymore. Like at the bench, I’ll be trying to work through an order, and then I’m suddenly checking a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny flaw, then a noise outside pulls me away, and when I come back to the piece I realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and reconstruct what I was doing.

So, yeah, it’s been going on at least a month, and it’s been pretty hard to ignore because my work makes every little lapse show up.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Over the past few weeks, it’s been pretty consistent. I wouldn’t say every single minute of the day, but most days it shows up enough that I notice it, especially once I’m trying to do anything that needs steady attention.

At the bench is where it’s most obvious. I’ll sit down thinking, okay, I’m going to finish this order, and then somehow I’m checking a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny thing on the piece, then a noise outside catches me, and when I look back down I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing. It’s like the work order just drops out of my head, and I have to piece it back together step by step.

So it’s not like it happened once or twice. It’s been repeating over these past weeks, enough that it feels like a real pattern now, and it’s frustrating because my work depends on staying with one thing carefully.

It felt like I had this motor in me and sitting still at the bench felt almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you been making impulsive decisions since your attention problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. Like, if you mean am I doing huge reckless things without thinking, I don’t think so… at least nothing that jumps out to me like, “Oh yeah, that was obviously impulsive.”\n\nI have been kind of jumping from thing to thing, and sometimes I’ll decide suddenly that something needs to be done right now, but whether that’s impulsive or just me being scattered and over-focused on whatever catches my attention, I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way. My main problem has felt more like I can’t keep my attention steady once I start something.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness been causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just something I noticed at work, but it’s started spilling over into everything.\n\nThe biggest thing was I missed a major custom delivery — like, a really important one, not just a small repair pickup or something I could smooth over easily. I had been moving between tasks and notes and little details, and somehow the whole thing got away from me. That’s not normal for me. I’m usually pretty careful with deadlines because people trust me with expensive and personal pieces, you know? And then my shop records got so messy — invoices, job notes, order details, all of it — that I basically had to stop taking new work for now because I couldn’t trust that I’d keep it straight.\n\nWith family, yeah, they’ve noticed. They’ve been checking on me more than usual, not in a casual “how are you” way, more like they’re worried. I think they could tell I’d slipped pretty far from my normal routines. I wasn’t keeping things organized the way I usually do, and I think that scared them a bit. So yes, it’s caused problems — maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where you didn't have these attention and restlessness problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, most of my life I haven’t been like this. I mean, I’ve always been busy and I can get absorbed in my work, but this specific wired, out-of-control feeling wasn’t just my usual personality.\n\nIt really started right after that jewelry-show award. I remember because it felt like everything suddenly sped up after that — like my head and my body both shifted into this different gear. From there it went on for about two weeks, give or take. During that stretch was when the attention problems and the restlessness felt really hard to control. Before that, I could sit and work normally, keep things in order, and not feel like I was being pulled in ten directions at once.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness lasted for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. That’s actually part of why it feels so strange to me — this wasn’t some long-running pattern I can trace back forever.\n\nThe wired, out-of-control stretch really started right after the jewelry-show award, and it went on for about two weeks. During that time it felt very intense, like I couldn’t get myself settled or keep my attention where it needed to be, but it wasn’t something that had been happening for months and months before that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely hasn’t been six months. I know I keep coming back to the same point, but that’s honestly how I’ve been marking it in my head — it started right after the jewelry-show award. That was the clear turning point for me.\n\nFrom there, that wired, out-of-control stretch went on for about two weeks. It felt intense while it was happening, like it took over my normal way of functioning, but it wasn’t something that had been there for half a year or anything close to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms of inattention or restlessness since the initial two-week period ended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since that main two-week stretch ended, I’d say the worst of it isn’t like it was, but I still notice this thing with my attention, especially at work.\n\nWhat really bothers me — and honestly what made me feel like I needed to come in — is that my mind won’t stay on one thing the way it used to. Like, I’ll be at the bench trying to finish a piece, and I’ll start checking a customer email, then suddenly I’m looking at a saw mark I left, then some noise outside catches me, and then I come back and realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the work I do needs focus. You can’t really be half in, half out when you’re dealing with small details and expensive materials. So, yes, I have still had some of that inattention or restlessness, just not as intense as that first stretch.\n\nDoctor: Are you still experiencing difficulties with attention and restlessness now?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Not like the worst part before, but it’s still there enough that it’s messing with me.\n\nThe main thing is my attention. What really brought me in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. At the bench, I’ll be trying to work through an order, and then I’m checking a customer email, then I notice some little saw mark or detail on a piece, then a noise outside pulls me away, and when I come back I realize I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing. Like I have to rebuild the whole work order in my head again.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because that’s not how I usually work. My job kind of depends on being precise and steady, so even if I’m physically sitting there, my mind keeps jumping around. That’s the part that still feels very much present right now.\n\nDoctor: Are your current symptoms of inattention something that you experience nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much nearly every day. It’s not always the exact same level every single day, but most days I notice it, especially when I’m at the bench trying to work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in — my mind just won’t stay on one thing the way it should. I’ll be working on a piece, then I jump to a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny detail I feel like I need to fix, then a noise outside pulls my attention, and by the time I look back down, I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. Then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, what step was I on, what was I supposed to do next, did I already check this part?\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s happening nearly every day, because my work makes it really obvious. If I had a different job maybe I could ignore it more, but with jewelry work, every little lapse stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention been present for at least one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way. I remember because it felt like everything suddenly sped up after that — like my head and my body both shifted into this different gear. From there it went on for about two weeks, give or take. During that stretch was when the attention problems and the restlessness felt really hard to control. Before that, I could sit and work normally, keep things in order, and not feel like I was being pulled in ten directions at once.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness lasted for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. That’s actually part of why it feels so strange to me — this wasn’t some long-running pattern I can trace back forever.\n\nThe wired, out-of-control stretch really started right after the jewelry-show award, and it went on for about two weeks. During that time it felt very intense, like I couldn’t get myself settled or keep my attention where it needed to be, but it wasn’t something that had been happening for months and months before that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely hasn’t been six months. I know I keep coming back to the same point, but that’s honestly how I’ve been marking it in my head — it started right after the jewelry-show award. That was the clear turning point for me.\n\nFrom there, that wired, out-of-control stretch went on for about two weeks. It felt intense while it was happening, like it took over my normal way of functioning, but it wasn’t something that had been there for half a year or anything close to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms of inattention or restlessness since the initial two-week period ended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since that main two-week stretch ended, I’d say the worst of it isn’t like it was, but I still notice this thing with my attention, especially at work.\n\nWhat really bothers me — and honestly what made me feel like I needed to come in — is that my mind won’t stay on one thing the way it used to. Like, I’ll be at the bench trying to finish a piece, and I’ll start checking a customer email, then suddenly I’m looking at a saw mark I left, then some noise outside catches me, and then I come back and realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the work I do needs focus. You can’t really be half in, half out when you’re dealing with small details and expensive materials. So, yes, I have still had some of that inattention or restlessness, just not as intense as that first stretch.\n\nDoctor: Are you still experiencing difficulties with attention and restlessness now?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Not like the worst part before, but it’s still there enough that it’s messing with me.\n\nThe main thing is my attention. What really brought me in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. At the bench, I’ll be trying to work through an order, and then I’m checking a customer email, then I notice some little saw mark or detail on a piece, then a noise outside pulls me away, and when I come back I realize I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing. Like I have to rebuild the whole work order in my head again.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because that’s not how I usually work. My job kind of depends on being precise and steady, so even if I’m physically sitting there, my mind keeps jumping around. That’s the part that still feels very much present right now.\n\nDoctor: Are your current symptoms of inattention something that you experience nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much nearly every day. It’s not always the exact same level every single day, but most days I notice it, especially when I’m at the bench trying to work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in — my mind just won’t stay on one thing the way it should. I’ll be working on a piece, then I jump to a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny detail I feel like I need to fix, then a noise outside pulls my attention, and by the time I look back down, I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. Then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, what step was I on, what was I supposed to do next, did I already check this part?\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s happening nearly every day, because my work makes it really obvious. If I had a different job maybe I could ignore it more, but with jewelry work, every little lapse stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention been present for at least one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. What really made me come in is that my mind won’t stay on one thing anymore. I wouldn’t say every single minute of the day, but most days it shows up enough that I notice it, especially once I’m trying to do anything that needs steady attention.\n\nAt the bench is where it’s most obvious. I’ll sit down thinking, okay, I’m going to finish this order, and then somehow I’m checking a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny thing on the piece, then a noise outside catches me, and when I look back down I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing. It’s like the work order just drops out of my head, and I have to piece it back together step by step.\n\nSo it’s not like it happened once or twice. It’s been repeating over these past weeks, enough that it feels like a real pattern now, and it’s frustrating because my work depends on staying with one thing carefully.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention been present for at least 6 months or is it less than that?\n\nPatient: Less than six months. It’s definitely not like this has been going on half a year.\n\nThe really wired, out-of-control stretch started right after I got that award at the jewelry show, and that went on for about two weeks. That’s the period where everything felt like it kind of kicked into gear and got hard to manage. So, no, I wouldn’t say six months — more like that couple-week stretch after the award was when it really showed up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been causing significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I missed a major custom delivery and my shop records got so disorganized that I had to stop taking new work for now. My family has also been checking on me more because they got worried about how far I’d slipped from my usual routines.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… definitely. I mean, I don’t really like saying that because my work is kind of the thing I’m usually proud of, but it’s been affecting my life in a real way.

I missed a major custom delivery, which is not like me. That kind of thing really matters in my shop because people are trusting me with something personal, and if I say it’s going to be done, I usually make sure it’s done. But I just couldn’t keep everything straight the way I normally do, and by the time I realized how far behind and scattered things had gotten, it was already a problem.

And my records got so disorganized that I actually had to stop taking new work for now. Like, I couldn’t keep adding orders on top of the mess because I didn’t trust myself to track everything properly. That’s embarrassing to admit, honestly, because it makes me feel like I’m losing control of something I’ve built.

My family’s noticed too. They’ve been checking on me more, asking if I’m okay, because I guess I’ve slipped pretty far from my usual routines. So, yes, it’s causing distress, and it’s definitely interfering with my day-to-day life and my work.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… the main thing is I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s getting in the way of work, mostly. I work at a bench with jewelry and small pieces, so normally I have to be really locked in, you know—like following a work order, checking a saw mark, answering a customer email, making sure I don’t miss some tiny detail.\n\nBut lately I’ll start on one thing, then I’m suddenly looking at an email, then I notice a mark on a piece, then some noise outside catches me, and then I look back and I’ve completely lost where I was in the work order. It’s like my attention just keeps jumping around before I can finish anything. That’s what made me think I should come in, because it’s not just annoying—it’s actually messing with my work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with keeping your mind on one thing?\n\nPatient: It really kicked in right after that jewelry-show award I got. Like, before that I’d have scattered days here and there, sure, but this was different — it felt like my brain got switched on too high and I couldn’t bring it back down. That stretch went on for about two weeks, maybe right around there. Two weeks of feeling wired and kind of out of control, and that’s when the trouble keeping my mind on one thing became really noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more active or restless than usual since your attention problems started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m normally active at work because it’s hands-on, but this was different. I couldn’t just sit at the bench and stay with one piece. I’d sit down to work on a setting or check a detail, and within minutes I’d be up again or grabbing my sketchbook because I suddenly had an idea for a new watch case. Then before that was even halfway sketched, I’d be emailing suppliers about materials, then I’d look over and decide the display trays looked wrong and start rearranging them.\n\nAnd this wasn’t just during normal hours. I’d still be doing it after closing, like I knew I should lock up, but I’d keep finding one more thing to move, one more idea to jot down, one more message to send. It felt like I had this motor in me and sitting still at the bench felt almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you been making impulsive decisions since your attention problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. Like, if you mean am I doing huge reckless things without thinking, I don’t think so… at least nothing that jumps out to me like, “Oh yeah, that was obviously impulsive.”\n\nI have been kind of jumping from thing to thing, and sometimes I’ll decide suddenly that something needs to be done right now, but whether that’s impulsive or just me being scattered and over-focused on whatever catches my attention, I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way. My main problem has felt more like I can’t keep my attention steady once I start something.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness been causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just something I noticed at work, but it’s started spilling over into everything.\n\nThe biggest thing was I missed a major custom delivery — like, a really important one, not just a small repair pickup or something I could smooth over easily. I had been moving between tasks and notes and little details, and somehow the whole thing got away from me. That’s not normal for me. I’m usually pretty careful with deadlines because people trust me with expensive and personal pieces, you know? And then my shop records got so messy — invoices, job notes, order details, all of it — that I basically had to stop taking new work for now because I couldn’t trust that I’d keep it straight.\n\nWith family, yeah, they’ve noticed. They’ve been checking on me more than usual, not in a casual “how are you” way, more like they’re worried. I think they could tell I’d slipped pretty far from my normal routines. I wasn’t keeping things organized the way I usually do, and I think that scared them a bit. So yes, it’s caused problems — maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where you didn't have these attention and restlessness problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, most of my life I haven’t been like this. I mean, I’ve always been busy and I can get absorbed in my work, but this specific wired, out-of-control feeling wasn’t just my usual personality.\n\nIt really started right after that jewelry-show award. During that stretch was when the attention problems and the restlessness felt really hard to control. That’s actually part of why it feels so strange to me — this wasn’t some long-running pattern I can trace back forever.\n\nThe wired, out-of-control stretch really started right after the jewelry-show award, and it went on for about two weeks. During that time it felt very intense, like I couldn’t get myself settled or keep my attention where it needed to be, but it wasn’t something that had been happening for months and months before that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely hasn’t been six months. I know I keep coming back to the same point, but that’s honestly how I’ve been marking it in my head — it started right after the jewelry-show award. That was the clear turning point for me.\n\nFrom there, that wired, out-of-control stretch went on for about two weeks. It felt intense while it was happening, like it took over my normal way of functioning, but it wasn’t something that had been there for half a year or anything close to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms of inattention or restlessness since the initial two-week period ended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since that main two-week stretch ended, I’d say the worst of it isn’t like it was, but I still notice this thing with my attention, especially at work.\n\nWhat really bothers me — and honestly what made me feel like I needed to come in — is that my mind won’t stay on one thing the way it used to. Like, I’ll be at the bench trying to finish a piece, and I’ll start checking a customer email, then suddenly I’m looking at a saw mark I left, then some noise outside catches me, and then I come back and realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the work I do needs focus. You can’t really be half in, half out when you’re dealing with small details and expensive materials. So, yes, I have still had some of that inattention or restlessness, just not as intense as that first stretch.\n\nDoctor: Are you still experiencing difficulties with attention and restlessness now?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Not like the worst part before, but it’s still there enough that it’s messing with me.\n\nThe main thing is my attention. What really brought me in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. At the bench, I’ll be trying to work through an order, and then I’m checking a customer email, then I notice some little saw mark or detail on a piece, then a noise outside pulls me away, and when I come back I realize I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing. Like I have to rebuild the whole work order in my head again.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because that’s not how I usually work. My job kind of depends on being precise and steady, so even if I’m physically sitting there, my mind keeps jumping around. Then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, what step was I on, what was I supposed to do next, did I already check this part?\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s happening nearly every day, because my work makes it really obvious. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it cleanly enough to say, like, “Yes, on this day it stopped and I was completely fine,” or anything like that.

There have probably been moments where I felt a little more settled, or where I wasn’t actively noticing it as much, especially if I wasn’t trying to do something that needed a lot of focus. But completely went away for a while? I don’t know. I can’t really say that with confidence.

It’s more like I’d realize after the fact that I’d been bouncing around or not finishing things, and then maybe I’d have a quieter patch, but I don’t know if that means the symptoms were gone or if I just wasn’t paying attention to them. So… I guess my honest answer is I haven’t noticed a clear period where it completely disappeared.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… the main thing is I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s getting in the way of work, mostly. It’s like my attention just keeps jumping around before I can finish anything. That’s what made me think I should come in, because it’s not just annoying—it’s actually messing with my work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with keeping your mind on one thing?\n\nPatient: It really kicked in right after that jewelry-show award I got. Like, before that I’d have scattered days here and there, sure, but this was different — it felt like my brain got switched on too high and I couldn’t bring it back down. That stretch went on for about two weeks, maybe right around there. Two weeks of feeling wired and kind of out of control, and that’s when the trouble keeping my mind on one thing became really noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more active or restless than usual since your attention problems started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m normally active at work because it’s hands-on, but this was different. I couldn’t just sit at the bench and stay with one piece. I’d sit down to work on a setting or check a detail, and within minutes I’d be up again or grabbing my sketchbook because I suddenly had an idea for a new watch case. Like, if you mean am I doing huge reckless things without thinking, I don’t think so… at least nothing that jumps out to me like, “Oh yeah, that was obviously impulsive.”\n\nI have been kind of jumping from thing to thing, and sometimes I’ll decide suddenly that something needs to be done right now, but whether that’s impulsive or just me being scattered and over-focused on whatever catches my attention, I don’t know. I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way. My main problem has felt more like I can’t keep my attention steady once I start something.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness been causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just something I noticed at work, but it’s started spilling over into everything.\n\nThe biggest thing was I missed a major custom delivery — like, a really important one, not just a small repair pickup or something I could smooth over easily. I’m usually pretty careful with deadlines because people trust me with expensive and personal pieces, you know? They’ve been checking on me more than usual, not in a casual “how are you” way, more like they’re worried. I think they could tell I’d slipped pretty far from my normal routines. I wasn’t keeping things organized the way I usually do, and I think that scared them a bit. So yes, it’s caused problems — maybe more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your life where you didn't have these attention and restlessness problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, most of my life I haven’t been like this. I mean, I’ve always been busy and I can get absorbed in my work, but this specific wired, out-of-control feeling wasn’t just my usual personality.\n\nIt really started right after that jewelry-show award. During that stretch was when the attention problems and the restlessness felt really hard to control. Before that, I could sit and work normally, keep things in order, and not feel like I was being pulled in ten directions at once.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness lasted for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. That’s actually part of why it feels so strange to me — this wasn’t some long-running pattern I can trace back forever.\n\nThe wired, out-of-control stretch really started right after the jewelry-show award, and it went on for about two weeks. During that time it felt very intense, like I couldn’t get myself settled or keep my attention where it needed to be, but it wasn’t something that had been happening for months and months before that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely hasn’t been six months. I know I keep coming back to the same point, but that’s honestly how I’ve been marking it in my head — it started right after the jewelry-show award. That was the clear turning point for me.\n\nFrom there, that wired, out-of-control stretch went on for about two weeks. It felt intense while it was happening, like it took over my normal way of functioning, but it wasn’t something that had been there for half a year or anything close to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms of inattention or restlessness since the initial two-week period ended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since that main two-week stretch ended, I’d say the worst of it isn’t like it was, but I still notice this thing with my attention, especially at work.\n\nWhat really bothers me — and honestly what made me feel like I needed to come in — is that my mind won’t stay on one thing the way it used to. Like, I’ll be at the bench trying to finish a piece, and I’ll start checking a customer email, then suddenly I’m looking at a saw mark I left, then some noise outside catches me, and then I come back and realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the work I do needs focus. You can’t really be half in, half out when you’re dealing with small details and expensive materials. So, yes, I have still had some of that inattention or restlessness, just not as intense as that first stretch.\n\nDoctor: Are you still experiencing difficulties with attention and restlessness now?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Not like the worst part before, but it’s still there enough that it’s messing with me.\n\nThe main thing is my attention. What really brought me in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. At the bench, I’ll be trying to work through an order, and then I’m checking a customer email, then I notice some little saw mark or detail on a piece, then a noise outside pulls me away, and when I come back I realize I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing. Like I have to rebuild the whole work order in my head again.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because that’s not how I usually work. My job kind of depends on being precise and steady, so even if I’m physically sitting there, my mind keeps jumping around. That’s the part that still feels very much present right now.\n\nDoctor: Are your current symptoms of inattention something that you experience nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much nearly every day. It’s not always the exact same level every single day, but most days I notice it, especially when I’m at the bench trying to work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in — my mind just won’t stay on one thing the way it should. I’ll be working on a piece, then I jump to a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny detail I feel like I need to fix, then a noise outside pulls my attention, and by the time I look back down, I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. Then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, what step was I on, what was I supposed to do next, did I already check this part?\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s happening nearly every day, because my work makes it really obvious. If I had a different job maybe I could ignore it more, but with jewelry work, every little lapse stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention been present for at least one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, it’s been long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad couple of days or me being distracted because of one stressful thing.\n\nFor at least a month now, my attention has just felt off. What really made me come in is that my mind won’t stay on one thing anymore. Like at the bench, I’ll be trying to work through an order, and then I’m suddenly checking a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny flaw, then a noise outside pulls me away, and when I come back to the piece I realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and reconstruct what I was doing.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s been going on at least a month, and it’s been pretty hard to ignore because my work makes every little lapse show up.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least six months is not applicable since you mentioned less than six months, but can you tell me more about how consistent these symptoms have been over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Over the past few weeks, it’s been pretty consistent. I wouldn’t say every single minute of the day, but most days it shows up enough that I notice it, especially once I’m trying to do anything that needs steady attention.\n\nAt the bench is where it’s most obvious. I’ll sit down thinking, okay, I’m going to finish this order, and then somehow I’m checking a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny thing on the piece, then a noise outside catches me, and when I look back down I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing. It’s like the work order just drops out of my head, and I have to piece it back together step by step.\n\nSo it’s not like it happened once or twice. It’s been repeating over these past weeks, enough that it feels like a real pattern now, and it’s frustrating because my work depends on staying with one thing carefully.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention been present for at least 6 months or is it less than that?\n\nPatient: Less than six months. It’s definitely not like this has been going on half a year.\n\nThe really wired, out-of-control stretch started right after I got that award at the jewelry show, and that went on for about two weeks. That’s the period where everything felt like it kind of kicked into gear and got hard to manage. I mean, I don’t really like saying that because my work is kind of the thing I’m usually proud of, but it’s been affecting my life in a real way.\n\nI missed a major custom delivery, which is not like me. That kind of thing really matters in my shop because people are trusting me with something personal, and if I say it’s going to be done, I usually make sure it’s done. But I just couldn’t keep everything straight the way I normally do, and by the time I realized how far behind and scattered things had gotten, it was already a problem.\n\nAnd my records got so disorganized that I actually had to stop taking new work for now. No, not more than a few months. I mean, I’ve had stressful times before, like anyone, especially with work deadlines, but this particular thing — the feeling wired and scattered and kind of out of control — that really started right after the jewelry-show award.

After the award, I got caught up in this stretch where I couldn’t settle myself or keep things organized the way I normally would, and that went on for about two weeks.

So if you’re asking whether it’s been months and months, no. It was more like that two-week period after the award where it became really obvious and hard to manage.

It’s been repeating over these past weeks, enough that it feels like a real pattern now, and it’s frustrating because my work depends on staying with one thing carefully.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention been present for at least 6 months or is it less than that?\n\nPatient: Less than six months. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I can say “completely back to normal,” exactly. That’s the part that’s hard for me to pin down.

There have been times where I felt calmer, or where I wasn’t noticing the scattered feeling as much, especially if I wasn’t trying to sit down and focus on something. But if you mean a stretch where I could clearly say, “Okay, I’m totally myself again, no restlessness, no attention problems at all,” I don’t think I’ve noticed that clearly.

It’s more like it comes in waves, or I only realize it afterward — like I’ll look back and think, “Was I actually settled then, or was I just not paying attention to it?” So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t had a period that stands out to me as completely symptom-free since it started.

I know I keep coming back to the same point, but that’s honestly how I’ve been marking it in my head — it started right after the jewelry-show award. That was the clear turning point for me.\n\nFrom there, that wired, out-of-control stretch went on for about two weeks. It felt intense while it was happening, like it took over my normal way of functioning, but it wasn’t something that had been there for half a year or anything close to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms of inattention or restlessness since the initial two-week period ended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since that main two-week stretch ended, I’d say the worst of it isn’t like it was, but I still notice this thing with my attention, especially at work.\n\nWhat really bothers me — and honestly what made me feel like I needed to come in — is that my mind won’t stay on one thing the way it used to. Like I have to rebuild the whole work order in my head again.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because that’s not how I usually work. My job kind of depends on being precise and steady, so even if I’m physically sitting there, my mind keeps jumping around. That’s the part that still feels very much present right now.\n\nDoctor: Are your current symptoms of inattention something that you experience nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much nearly every day. I remember because it felt like everything suddenly sped up after that — like my head and my body both shifted into this different gear. From there it went on for about two weeks, give or take. During that stretch was when the attention problems and the restlessness felt really hard to control. Before that, I could sit and work normally, keep things in order, and not feel like I was being pulled in ten directions at once.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness lasted for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. That’s actually part of why it feels so strange to me — this wasn’t some long-running pattern I can trace back forever.\n\nThe wired, out-of-control stretch really started right after the jewelry-show award, and it went on for about two weeks. During that time it felt very intense, like I couldn’t get myself settled or keep my attention where it needed to be, but it wasn’t something that had been happening for months and months before that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely hasn’t been six months. I know I keep coming back to the same point, but that’s honestly how I’ve been marking it in my head — it started right after the jewelry-show award. That was the clear turning point for me.\n\nFrom there, that wired, out-of-control stretch went on for about two weeks. It felt intense while it was happening, like it took over my normal way of functioning, but it wasn’t something that had been there for half a year or anything close to that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any symptoms of inattention or restlessness since the initial two-week period ended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since that main two-week stretch ended, I’d say the worst of it isn’t like it was, but I still notice this thing with my attention, especially at work.\n\nWhat really bothers me — and honestly what made me feel like I needed to come in — is that my mind won’t stay on one thing the way it used to. Like, I’ll be at the bench trying to finish a piece, and I’ll start checking a customer email, then suddenly I’m looking at a saw mark I left, then some noise outside catches me, and then I come back and realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and figure out where I even was.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the work I do needs focus. You can’t really be half in, half out when you’re dealing with small details and expensive materials. So, yes, I have still had some of that inattention or restlessness, just not as intense as that first stretch.\n\nDoctor: Are you still experiencing difficulties with attention and restlessness now?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am. Not like the worst part before, but it’s still there enough that it’s messing with me.\n\nThe main thing is my attention. What really brought me in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. At the bench, I’ll be trying to work through an order, and then I’m checking a customer email, then I notice some little saw mark or detail on a piece, then a noise outside pulls me away, and when I come back I realize I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing. Like I have to rebuild the whole work order in my head again.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because that’s not how I usually work. My job kind of depends on being precise and steady, so even if I’m physically sitting there, my mind keeps jumping around. That’s the part that still feels very much present right now.\n\nDoctor: Are your current symptoms of inattention something that you experience nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much nearly every day. It’s not always the exact same level every single day, but most days I notice it, especially when I’m at the bench trying to work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in — my mind just won’t stay on one thing the way it should. I’ll be working on a piece, then I jump to a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny detail I feel like I need to fix, then a noise outside pulls my attention, and by the time I look back down, I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. Then I’m sitting there trying to remember, okay, what step was I on, what was I supposed to do next, did I already check this part?\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s happening nearly every day, because my work makes it really obvious. If I had a different job maybe I could ignore it more, but with jewelry work, every little lapse stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention been present for at least one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I mean, it’s been long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad couple of days or me being distracted because of one stressful thing.\n\nFor at least a month now, my attention has just felt off. What really made me come in is that my mind won’t stay on one thing anymore. Like at the bench, I’ll be trying to work through an order, and then I’m suddenly checking a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny flaw, then a noise outside pulls me away, and when I come back to the piece I realize I’ve lost the whole work order in my head. I have to stop and reconstruct what I was doing.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s been going on at least a month, and it’s been pretty hard to ignore because my work makes every little lapse show up.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least six months is not applicable since you mentioned less than six months, but can you tell me more about how consistent these symptoms have been over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Over the past few weeks, it’s been pretty consistent. I wouldn’t say every single minute of the day, but most days it shows up enough that I notice it, especially once I’m trying to do anything that needs steady attention.\n\nAt the bench is where it’s most obvious. I’ll sit down thinking, okay, I’m going to finish this order, and then somehow I’m checking a customer email, then I notice a saw mark or some tiny thing on the piece, then a noise outside catches me, and when I look back down I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing. It’s like the work order just drops out of my head, and I have to piece it back together step by step.\n\nSo it’s not like it happened once or twice. It’s been repeating over these past weeks, enough that it feels like a real pattern now, and it’s frustrating because my work depends on staying with one thing carefully.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention been present for at least 6 months or is it less than that?\n\nPatient: Less than six months. It’s definitely not like this has been going on half a year.\n\nThe really wired, out-of-control stretch started right after I got that award at the jewelry show, and that went on for about two weeks. That’s the period where everything felt like it kind of kicked into gear and got hard to manage. So, no, I wouldn’t say six months — more like that couple-week stretch after the award was when it really showed up.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been causing significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I don’t really like saying that because my work is kind of the thing I’m usually proud of, but it’s been affecting my life in a real way.\n\nI missed a major custom delivery, which is not like me. That kind of thing really matters in my shop because people are trusting me with something personal, and if I say it’s going to be done, I usually make sure it’s done. But I just couldn’t keep everything straight the way I normally do, and by the time I realized how far behind and scattered things had gotten, it was already a problem.\n\nAnd my records got so disorganized that I actually had to stop taking new work for now. That’s embarrassing to admit, honestly, because it makes me feel like I’m losing control of something I’ve built.\n\nMy family’s noticed too. They’ve been checking on me more, asking if I’m okay, because I guess I’ve slipped pretty far from my usual routines. So, yes, it’s causing distress, and it’s definitely interfering with my day-to-day life and my work.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention been present for at least 6 months or is it less than that?\n\nPatient: It’s less than six months.
